Perfect! Now I have all the information I need. Let me compile my findings:

## Desloppify Exclude Mechanism Research

### 1. **CLI Command - `exclude` (desloppify/app/commands/exclude.py)**

The `cmd_exclude` function:
- Takes a path pattern as an argument
- Calls `config_mod.add_exclude_pattern(config, args.pattern)` to add the pattern
- Sets `config["needs_rescan"] = True` to mark cached data as stale
- Saves the config to disk
- **Cleans up affected state**: Prunes any issues whose file paths match the new exclusion from `state["issues"]`
- **Updates the living plan**: Removes the pruned issue IDs from the plan's queue/skips/clusters using `purge_ids()`

### 2. **Exclusion Storage (.desloppify/config.json)**

From `base/config.py`:
- Exclusions are stored as a **list of strings** in `config["exclude"]` (line 64)
- ConfigKey definition: `"exclude": ConfigKey(list, [], "Path patterns to exclude from scanning")`
- The `add_exclude_pattern()` function (lines 210-214) deduplicates and appends new patterns
- Config is saved atomically using `safe_write_text()` with temp file + os.replace

Example structure in `.desloppify/config.json`:
```json
{
  "exclude": [".claude/worktrees", "node_modules", "dist"],
  ...
}
```

### 3. **matches_exclusion() Function (base/discovery/file_paths.py)**

This is the **core matching logic** (lines 13-32):

```python
def matches_exclusion(rel_path: str, exclusion: str) -> bool:
    """Check if a relative path matches an exclusion pattern."""
    parts = Path(rel_path).parts
    
    # 1. Exact component match: ".claude/worktrees" matches ANY path containing it
    if exclusion in parts:
        return True
    
    # 2. Wildcard patterns:
    #    - Per-component: "*.py" matches "foo.py" in any component
    #    - Full path: "foo/**" matches "foo/anything/deeper"
    if "*" in exclusion:
        if any(fnmatch.fnmatch(part, exclusion) for part in parts):
            return True
        if "/" in exclusion or os.sep in exclusion:
            normalized_path = rel_path.lstrip("./")
            if fnmatch.fnmatch(normalized_path, exclusion):
                return True
    
    # 3. Path prefix match: ".claude/worktrees" matches full path prefix
    if "/" in exclusion or os.sep in exclusion:
        normalized = exclusion.rstrip("/").rstrip(os.sep)
        return rel_path == normalized or \
               rel_path.startswith(normalized + "/") or \
               rel_path.startswith(normalized + os.sep)
    
    return False
```

### 4. **What `desloppify exclude .claude/worktrees` Does**

1. **Adds the pattern** `.claude/worktrees` to `config["exclude"]` in `.desloppify/config.json`
2. **Marks for rescan**: Sets `needs_rescan = True`
3. **Immediately prunes state**: 
   - Finds all issues with file paths in `.desloppify/worktrees/` (matches via `matches_exclusion()`)
   - Removes them from `state["issues"]`
   - Saves the cleaned state file
4. **Updates the plan**: Removes those issue IDs from the living plan's queue, skips, and clusters
5. **Displays progress**: Shows how many issues were removed

**Matching behavior for `.claude/worktrees`:**
- `matches_exclusion("some/path", ".claude/worktrees")` checks if the path starts with `.claude/worktrees/` (prefix match via line 29-31)
- Files like `.claude/worktrees/decompositions/file.py` ✓ matched
- Files like `.desloppify/worktrees/...` ✗ NOT matched (different prefix)

### 5. **Import Path in CLI**

From `cli.py` lines 75-94:
- CLI loads persisted exclusions from config
- Calls `set_exclusions(combined)` in `base/discovery/source.py` to apply them globally before scanning
- Prints which exclusions are active (from config or CLI flags)

This ensures all subsequent scans respect the exclusion list without needing to manually re-specify them each time.

    46→def set_exclusions(patterns: list[str]) -> None:
    47→    """Set global exclusion patterns (called once from CLI at startup)."""
    48→    runtime = current_runtime_context()
    49→    runtime.exclusions = tuple(patterns)

    59→    """Enable scan-scoped file content cache."""
    60→    runtime = current_runtime_context()
    61→    runtime.file_text_cache.enable()

    66→    """Disable file content cache and free memory."""
    67→    runtime = current_runtime_context()

    69→    runtime.cache_enabled = False
    70→
    71→
    72→@contextmanager
    73→def file_cache_scope():
    74→    """Temporarily enable file cache within a context, with nested safety."""
    75→    runtime = current_runtime_context()
    76→    was_enabled = runtime.cache_enabled
    77→    if not was_enabled:
    78→        enable_file_cache()
    79→    try:
    80→        yield
    81→    finally:
    82→        if not was_enabled:
    83→            disable_file_cache()
    84→

    87→    """Return whether scan-scoped file cache is currently enabled."""

    92→    """Read a file as text, with optional caching."""

    99→
   100→def collect_exclude_dirs(scan_root: Path) -> list[str]:
   101→    """All exclusion directories as absolute paths, for passing to external tools.
   102→
   103→    Combines DEFAULT_EXCLUSIONS (non-glob entries) + get_exclusions() (runtime/config),
   104→    resolves each against *scan_root*. Filters out glob patterns (``*`` in name)
   105→    since most CLI tools want plain directory paths.
   106→    """
   107→    patterns = set()
   108→    for pat in DEFAULT_EXCLUSIONS:
   109→        if "*" not in pat:
   110→            patterns.add(pat)
   111→    patterns.update(p for p in get_exclusions() if p and "*" not in p)
   112→    return [str(scan_root / p) for p in sorted(patterns) if p]
   113→
   114→
   115→def _is_excluded_dir(name: str, rel_path: str, extra: tuple[str, ...]) -> bool:
   116→    in_default_exclusions = name in DEFAULT_EXCLUSIONS or name.endswith(".egg-info")
   117→    is_virtualenv_dir = name.startswith(".venv") or name.startswith("venv")
   118→    matches_extra_exclusion = bool(
   119→        extra
   120→        and any(
   121→            matches_exclusion(rel_path, exclusion)
   122→            or exclusion == name
   123→            or exclusion == name + "/**"
   124→            or exclusion == name + "/*"
   125→            for exclusion in extra
   126→        )
   127→    )
   128→    return in_default_exclusions or is_virtualenv_dir or matches_extra_exclusion
   129→
   130→
   131→def _find_source_files_cached(
   132→    path: str,
   133→    extensions: tuple[str, ...],
   134→    exclusions: tuple[str, ...] | None = None,
   135→    extra_exclusions: tuple[str, ...] = (),
   136→) -> tuple[str, ...]:
   137→    """Cached file discovery using os.walk with traversal-time pruning."""
   138→    cache_key = (path, extensions, exclusions, extra_exclusions)
   139→    cache = current_runtime_context().source_file_cache
   140→    cached = cache.get(cache_key)
   141→    if cached is not None:
   142→        return cached
   143→
   144→    project_root = get_project_root()
   145→    root = Path(path)
   146→    if not root.is_absolute():
   147→        root = project_root / root
   148→    all_exclusions = (exclusions or ()) + extra_exclusions
   149→    ext_set = set(extensions)
   150→    files: list[str] = []
   151→    for dirpath, dirnames, filenames in os.walk(root):
   152→        rel_dir = _normalize_path_separators(_safe_relpath(dirpath, project_root))
   153→        dirnames[:] = sorted(
   154→            d
   155→            for d in dirnames
   156→            if not _is_excluded_dir(d, rel_dir + "/" + d, all_exclusions)
   157→        )
   158→        for fname in filenames:
   159→            if any(fname.endswith(ext) for ext in ext_set):
   160→                full = os.path.join(dirpath, fname)
   161→                rel_file = _normalize_path_separators(_safe_relpath(full, project_root))
   162→                if all_exclusions and any(
   163→                    matches_exclusion(rel_file, ex) for ex in all_exclusions
   164→                ):
   165→                    continue
   166→                files.append(rel_file)
   167→    result = tuple(sorted(files))
   168→    cache.put(cache_key, result)
   169→    return result
   170→
   171→
   172→def find_source_files(
   173→    path: str | Path, extensions: list[str], exclusions: list[str] | None = None
   174→) -> list[str]:
   175→    """Find all files with given extensions under a path, excluding patterns."""
   176→    return list(
   177→        _find_source_files_cached(
   178→            str(path),
   179→            tuple(extensions),
   180→            tuple(exclusions) if exclusions else None,
   181→            get_exclusions(),
   182→        )
   183→    )
   184→
   185→
   186→def find_ts_files(path: str | Path) -> list[str]:
   187→    return find_source_files(path, [".ts", ".tsx"])
   188→
   189→
   190→def find_tsx_files(path: str | Path) -> list[str]:
   191→    return find_source_files(path, [".tsx"])
   192→
   193→
   194→def find_py_files(path: str | Path) -> list[str]:
   195→    return find_source_files(path, [".py"])
   196→

   198→__all__ = [
   199→    "DEFAULT_EXCLUSIONS",

   201→    "set_exclusions",

   204→    "disable_file_cache",

Now let me examine the TypeScript phases.py more carefully to identify the monster functions at lines 219 and 424.

Based on my thorough analysis of the four files, here is a comprehensive breakdown of each monster function:

---

## 1. `_add_review_parser()` in parser_groups_admin_review.py (lines 8–287)

### What it does
Builds the complete argument parser for the `desloppify review` command by adding 6 nested argument groups with dozens of individual flags. It defines options for:
- Core review operations (prepare, import, validate, dimensions)
- External review workflows (cloud-based reviewer sessions)
- Batch execution (parallelization, timeouts, retries)
- Trust & attestation (verification of review provenance)
- Post-processing (merging duplicate issues)

### Line count
**280 lines** (lines 8–287, excluding the module docstring and imports)

### Logical sub-sections that could be extracted

1. **Core options group** (lines 24–84): 60 lines — Basic path, state, prepare/import flags, dimensions, retrospective settings
2. **External review group** (lines 87–121): 34 lines — `--external-start`, `--external-submit`, session config
3. **Batch execution group** (lines 124–240): 116 lines — Runner, parallelization, timeouts, retries, heartbeats, stall detection, logging
4. **Trust & attestation group** (lines 243–270): 27 lines — Override, attestation, blind provenance flags
5. **Post-processing group** (lines 273–284): 11 lines — Merge and similarity threshold

### Complexity & decomposition risk
**Low-Medium Risk**

**Pros:**
- Each group is self-contained with no cross-dependencies
- Simple pattern: `g_* = p_review.add_argument_group(...)` followed by `.add_argument()` calls
- No business logic, purely declarative parser setup
- Each function would be a straightforward ~30-60 line builder

**Cons:**
- The function is a facade; it would multiply the number of public functions
- Each extracted function would need a similar signature: `(p_review) -> None`
- Risk: Maintainers might forget to call a new extracted function, breaking the parser

**Recommendation:** Extract into 5 small helper functions (`_add_core_options()`, `_add_external_review_options()`, `_add_batch_execution_options()`, `_add_trust_options()`, `_add_postprocessing_options()`). Straightforward refactoring with minimal risk if extracted functions are **called explicitly** in `_add_review_parser()`.

---

## 2. `add_plan_parser()` in parser_groups_plan_impl.py (lines 8–366)

### What it does
Builds the argument parser for the `desloppify plan` command and all of its subcommands. It defines 11 subparsers (show, queue, reset, reorder, describe, note, skip, unskip, reopen, resolve, focus, cluster, triage, commit-log) with their own nested options. Handles complex help text and examples.

### Line count
**358 lines** (lines 8–366, excluding module docstring/imports)

### Logical sub-sections that could be extracted

1. **Main plan parser setup** (lines 9–51): 42 lines — Description, epilog, formatter, base arguments
2. **Simple subcommands** (lines 54–68): 14 lines — show, queue, reset (short parsers)
3. **Reorder/position commands** (lines 70–100): 30 lines — reorder with target/position logic
4. **Describe/note/skip/unskip/reopen** (lines 102–162): 60 lines — Pattern-based bulk operations
5. **Resolve command** (lines 164–204): 40 lines — Complex attestation and skip logic
6. **Focus command** (lines 206–209): 3 lines — Trivial
7. **Cluster subcommand family** (lines 211–272): 61 lines — Create, add, remove, delete, reorder, show, list, merge, update
8. **Triage nested flags** (lines 274–336): 62 lines — Helper function + triage subparser (multi-stage workflow)
9. **Commit-log subcommand family** (lines 338–363): 25 lines — record, history, pr subparsers

### Complexity & decomposition risk
**Medium Risk**

**Pros:**
- Groups are logically separated by command family
- Triage flags are already extracted as an internal helper function `_add_triage_flags()`
- Cluster management is a cohesive block
- Commit-log is self-contained

**Cons:**
- The function must maintain a single `plan_sub` reference for all subparsers — extracting functions need that reference
- Cluster and commit-log subcommands have nested `add_subparsers()` calls (complex nesting)
- Dense argument definitions make visual tracking hard
- Risk of missing an extracted function call or argument

**Recommendation:** Extract into 6–7 helpers:
1. `_add_simple_subparsers()` — show, queue, reset
2. `_add_positioning_subparsers()` — reorder
3. `_add_bulk_operation_subparsers()` — describe, note, skip, unskip, reopen
4. `_add_resolve_subparser()` — resolve with attestation
5. `_add_focus_subparser()` — focus
6. `_add_cluster_subparser()` — cluster family (already complex due to nested structure)
7. `_add_commit_log_subparser()` — commit-log family

Already has the `_add_triage_flags()` pattern, so adding `_add_*_subparser()` functions would be consistent. **Medium risk** due to nested subparser dependencies.

---

## 3. `do_run_batches()` in execution.py (lines 44–549)

### What it does
Orchestrates the entire batch execution workflow for the review command:
- Validates runner and loads/prepares the batch packet
- Sets up batch execution options (parallelization, timeouts, retries)
- Builds and tracks batch tasks with a live progress callback
- Executes batches (in parallel or serial) with heartbeat monitoring
- Collects results, handles failures/partial completion
- Merges batch outputs and imports issues into state
- Optionally runs follow-up scan

This is a **state machine orchestrator** managing the full review execution pipeline.

### Line count
**505 lines** (lines 44–549, excluding the helper function `_record_execution_issue` at lines 37–42)

### Logical sub-sections that could be extracted

1. **Argument unpacking & policy resolution** (lines 70–82): 12 lines — Extract runner, allow_partial, policy settings
2. **Packet loading & dimension validation** (lines 84–108): 24 lines — Load packet, normalize dims, validate batches
3. **Batch selection & runtime calculation** (lines 110–124): 14 lines — Selected indexes, worker count, waves, ETA
4. **Artifact preparation** (lines 125–152): 27 lines — Create run dir, logs, prepare prompts, write run log
5. **Dry-run early exit** (lines 154–164): 10 lines — Check dry-run flag, log, return
6. **Progress reporting closure** (lines 174–319): 145 lines — `_report_progress()` nested function with heartbeat, batch start/done events, stall warnings
7. **Batch execution** (lines 357–384): 27 lines — Call execute_batches_fn, handle KeyboardInterrupt
8. **Result collection & status mapping** (lines 386–414): 28 lines — Collect results, classify failures, update batch_status
9. **Failure handling** (lines 416–448): 32 lines — Check allow_partial, print failures, partial import decision
10. **Merge & import** (lines 450–537): 87 lines — Merge results, collect reviewed files, build provenance, dimension coverage, import issues
11. **Follow-up scan** (lines 539–548): 9 lines — Conditional scan after import

### Complexity & decomposition risk
**High Risk**

**Pros:**
- Clear phases: setup → execution → collection → merge → import → scan
- Progress reporting is a self-contained closure that could move to a helper
- Result collection and status mapping are distinct steps
- Failure handling is modular

**Cons:**
- **Heavy state interdependence**: Variables like `batch_status`, `append_run_log`, `batch_positions`, `selected_indexes`, `run_dir`, `logs_dir`, etc. are shared across many closures
- The `_report_progress()` closure (145 lines) captures 20+ outer variables; extracting it would require passing many parameters
- Import step (lines 516–536) has try/except for SystemExit and general Exception with different logging — can't easily extract
- Function has 13 named dependencies injected as kwargs — each extracted function would need a subset of these
- **Runtime artifacts** (paths, run_log writer, batch status dict) are mutated throughout; refactoring requires careful state threading

**Recommendation:** **Extract with caution**. Best candidates:

1. **`_build_progress_reporter()`** — Extract the `_report_progress()` closure into a standalone function that returns the closure. Signature: `(batch_positions, selected_indexes, total_batches, append_run_log, colorize_fn, stall_warning_seconds) -> Callable`. This reduces the monster function to ~360 lines.

2. **`_map_batch_results_to_status()`** — Extract lines 386–414 (result collection logic). Signature: `(selected_indexes, output_files, batch_results, failures, batch_status, batch_positions) -> (successful_indexes, failure_set, batch_status)`.

3. **`_write_merged_import_data()`** — Extract lines 450–512 (merge, dimension coverage, provenance, safe write). Signature: `(batch_results, batches, selected_indexes, scored_dimensions, packet_dimensions, ...) -> merged_dict`.

4. **`_execute_and_log_import()`** — Extract lines 516–536 (the import try/except block).

5. **`_run_optional_followup_scan()`** — Extract lines 539–548.

Extracted functions would still need 5–10 parameters each. **Feasibility: 6/10**. The interconnected state and multiple closures make this risky without comprehensive refactoring.

---

## 4. `phase_structural()` in typescript/phases.py (lines 219–369)

### What it does
Orchestrates structural analysis of TypeScript code by running 5 detector families and aggregating findings:
1. Large files detection
2. Complexity scoring with weighted signals
3. God component detection (excessive hooks)
4. Mixed concern detection
5. Flat directory detection
6. Prop interface bloat detection
7. Passthrough component detection

Returns merged signals into issues (using structural signal merging) plus flat dirs and props-specific issues.

### Line count
**150 lines** (lines 219–369, excluding function signature/docstring)

### Logical sub-sections that could be extracted

1. **Large files** (lines 224–232): 8 lines — Detect, add to structural dict
2. **Complexity** (lines 234–247): 13 lines — Detect with signals, store score in lang.complexity_map
3. **Gods** (lines 249–261): 12 lines — Detect god components, add to structural
4. **Concerns** (lines 263–270): 7 lines — Detect mixed concerns, add to structural
5. **Merge structural signals** (lines 272): 1 line — Standalone call to merge_structural_signals()
6. **Flat directories** (lines 274–310): 36 lines — Detect, iterate entries, create issues, log
7. **Props bloat** (lines 313–340): 27 lines — Detect, tiered severity, create issues
8. **Passthrough components** (lines 342–363): 21 lines — Detect, create issues
9. **Potentials aggregation** (lines 364–369): 5 lines — Build return dict

### Complexity & decomposition risk
**Low-Medium Risk**

**Pros:**
- Each detector (large, complexity, gods, concerns) is independent — could become helpers
- Flat dirs, props, and passthrough are already independent blocks
- Each creates issues from detector output following the same pattern
- `lang` parameter is passed to most helpers; each extracted function could have signature `(path, lang) -> list[Issue]`
- No cross-dependencies between sections

**Cons:**
- The `structural` dict is built up across 4 sections (large, complexity, gods, concerns) then merged — can't extract those 4 independently without returning partial dicts
- Return type is complex: `(list[Issue], dict[str, int])` — extracted functions need consistent returns
- `lang.complexity_map` and `lang.zone_map` are mutated; extracted functions would mutate outer state

**Recommendation:** Extract into **logical phase sub-functions**:

1. **`_phase_structural_signals()`** (lines 222–272): 50 lines — Detect large, complexity, gods, concerns and merge. Signature: `(path, lang) -> list[Issue]`.

2. **`_phase_flat_dirs()`** (lines 274–310): 36 lines — Flat dir issues. Signature: `(path, lang) -> tuple[list[Issue], int]`.

3. **`_phase_props_bloat()`** (lines 313–340): 27 lines — Props issues. Signature: `(path, lang) -> tuple[list[Issue], int]`.

4. **`_phase_passthrough_components()`** (lines 342–363): 21 lines — Passthrough issues. Signature: `(path, lang) -> tuple[list[Issue], int]`.

Then `phase_structural()` becomes a ~20-line orchestrator:
```python
def phase_structural(path: Path, lang: LangRuntimeContract):
    results = _phase_structural_signals(path, lang)
    flat_issues, flat_count = _phase_flat_dirs(path, lang)
    results.extend(flat_issues)
    prop_issues, prop_count = _phase_props_bloat(path, lang)
    results.extend(prop_issues)
    pass_issues, pass_count = _phase_passthrough_components(path, lang)
    results.extend(pass_issues)
    potentials = {...}
    return results, potentials
```

**Feasibility: 8/10**. Straightforward extraction with clear boundaries.

---

## 5. `phase_coupling()` in typescript/phases.py (lines 424–580)

### What it does
Orchestrates dependency graph and coupling analysis by running 7 detector families and aggregating findings:
1. Single-use abstractions (shared helpers only used by one consumer)
2. Coupling violations (shared imports backwards from tools)
3. Boundary candidates (single-importer but sizeable files)
4. Cross-tool imports (violations of tool isolation)
5. Cycles (circular dependencies)
6. Orphaned files (no importers)
7. Re-export facades (unnecessary barrel re-exports)
8. Pattern anomalies (competing patterns in same area)
9. Naming inconsistencies (mixed naming conventions)

Returns aggregated issues and potential counts.

### Line count
**156 lines** (lines 424–580, excluding function signature/docstring)

### Logical sub-sections that could be extracted

1. **Prep: Build dep graph, set zone_map** (lines 426–428): 3 lines
2. **Single-use** (lines 430–441): 11 lines — Detect, filter, create issues
3. **Boundary candidates** (lines 442–470): 28 lines — Helpers for shared/tools prefix, detect boundaries, deduplicate against single-use
4. **Cross-tool imports** (lines 472–495): 23 lines — Detect, create issues, log
5. **Cycles** (lines 497–500): 3 lines — Detect, create issues
6. **Orphaned files** (lines 501–513): 12 lines — Detect with options, create issues
7. **Re-export facades** (lines 515–518): 3 lines — Detect, create issues
8. **Pattern anomalies** (lines 520–540): 20 lines — Detect, iterate, create issues
9. **Naming inconsistencies** (lines 542–567): 25 lines — Detect, iterate, create issues
10. **Potentials aggregation** (lines 568–580): 12 lines — Collect edge counts, build return dict

### Complexity & decomposition risk
**Medium-High Risk**

**Pros:**
- Most sections are independent: single-use, cycles, orphaned, facades, patterns, naming
- Each follows a pattern: detect → filter → iterate → make_issue
- Could extract 6–7 independent phase sub-functions

**Cons:**
- **Boundary candidates** (lines 442–470) depends on `single_entries` from the single-use phase — can't extract independently
- **Result accumulation**: All 7 detector branches `results.extend()` into a shared list — extraction requires consistent returns
- **Edge count aggregation** (lines 569–570): `coupling_edge_counts` and `cross_edge_counts` are used in potentials calculation — must be returned from extracted functions
- `lang.zone_map` is used heavily for filtering — mutates state indirectly through calls to `filter_entries()`
- The `_make_boundary_issues()` helper already exists but is only called once — indicates this function is tightly coupled

**Recommendation:** Extract into **phase blocks** with shared dependencies:

1. **`_phase_single_use_and_boundaries()`** (lines 430–470): 40 lines — Combine single-use and boundary-candidate detection. Signature: `(path, graph, lang, shared_prefix, tools_prefix) -> tuple[list[Issue], int, dict]`.

2. **`_phase_cross_tool_imports()`** (lines 472–495): 23 lines — Cross-tool issues. Signature: `(path, graph, lang, tools_prefix) -> tuple[list[Issue], dict]`.

3. **`_phase_cycles()`** (lines 497–500): 3 lines — Cycles. Signature: `(graph, lang) -> list[Issue]`.

4. **`_phase_orphaned()`** (lines 501–513): 12 lines — Orphaned issues. Signature: `(path, graph, lang) -> list[Issue]`.

5. **`_phase_facades()`** (lines 515–518): 3 lines — Facades. Signature: `(graph, lang) -> list[Issue]`.

6. **`_phase_patterns()`** (lines 520–540): 20 lines — Pattern anomalies. Signature: `(path) -> list[Issue]`.

7. **`_phase_naming()`** (lines 542–567): 25 lines — Naming. Signature: `(path, lang) -> list[Issue]`.

Then `phase_coupling()` becomes a ~40-line orchestrator:
```python
def phase_coupling(path, lang):
    results = []
    graph = deps_detector_mod.build_dep_graph(path)
    lang.dep_graph = graph
    
    # Single-use + boundaries (depends on single_entries)
    su_issues, su_count, edge_counts1 = _phase_single_use_and_boundaries(...)
    results.extend(su_issues)
    
    # Cross-tool
    ct_issues, edge_counts2 = _phase_cross_tool_imports(...)
    results.extend(ct_issues)
    
    # Cycles, orphaned, facades, patterns, naming
    results.extend(_phase_cycles(...))
    results.extend(_phase_orphaned(...))
    results.extend(_phase_facades(...))
    results.extend(_phase_patterns(...))
    results.extend(_phase_naming(...))
    
    potentials = {...}
    return results, potentials
```

**Feasibility: 6/10**. The boundary-candidates dependency on single-use and edge-count threading make this moderately complex, but doable. The existing `_make_boundary_issues()` helper shows the pattern is acceptable here.

---

## Summary Table

| Function | File | Lines | Extract Risk | Best Approach |
|----------|------|-------|--------------|---------------|
| `_add_review_parser()` | parser_groups_admin_review.py | 280 | **Low** | 5 small helpers (~60 lines each) |
| `add_plan_parser()` | parser_groups_plan_impl.py | 358 | **Medium** | 6–7 subparser builders (~50 lines each) |
| `do_run_batches()` | execution.py | 505 | **High** | Extract progress callback + import/merge blocks; heavy state sharing |
| `phase_structural()` | phases.py (219–369) | 150 | **Low-Medium** | 4 phase sub-functions; clear boundaries |
| `phase_coupling()` | phases.py (424–580) | 156 | **Medium-High** | 7 phase blocks; boundary-candidate dependency complicates extraction |

---

All four are candidates for decomposition, but with varying difficulty. The parser builders are **safest** (low interdependence), while `do_run_batches()` has the **highest risk** due to shared closures and state mutations.

## TEST DEBT RESEARCH REPORT

Based on my thorough analysis of the desloppify codebase, here is the detailed research on the 5 test debt issues:

---

### 1. UNTESTED MODULE: `desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/reflect_dashboard.py`

**What the module does:**
This is a small utility module (82 lines) containing 4 private functions that render the "reflect stage" dashboard display for the epic triage workflow:
- `_print_reflect_dashboard(si, plan)` - Main orchestrator that displays completed clusters, resolved issues, and recurring patterns
- `_print_completed_clusters(completed)` - Shows clusters completed in previous triage runs
- `_print_resolved_issues(resolved)` - Shows issues that have been fixed since last triage
- `_print_recurring_patterns(open_issues, resolved)` - Detects and displays dimensions with both resolved AND open issues (indicating potential recurring loops)

**Location:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/reflect_dashboard.py`

**Current testing status:** **Zero tests** - This module is completely untested. The function is actually used in `display.py` (line 426) in `cmd_triage_dashboard()`, but there are no dedicated tests.

**What's missing:**
1. No tests for the main `_print_reflect_dashboard()` function behavior
2. No tests for cluster completion display formatting
3. No tests for resolved issues filtering and display
4. No tests for `detect_recurring_patterns()` integration (called from `epic_triage.py`)
5. No tests for the "first triage" messaging path (when no prior work exists)
6. No edge case coverage: empty lists, None values, truncation at 10 items, etc.

**What testing is needed:**
- **Unit tests** verifying each helper function's output formatting
  - Test `_print_completed_clusters()` with 0, 1, 5, and 11+ clusters (tests the truncation at 10)
  - Test `_print_resolved_issues()` with varying resolved counts and issue structures
  - Test `_print_recurring_patterns()` correctly detects dimensions with both resolved and open issues
  - Test colorization and formatting is applied correctly
- **Integration tests** with `detect_recurring_patterns()` from `epic_triage.py`
  - Test with realistic state/issue data structures
  - Test dimension extraction from issue detail dicts
- **Edge cases:** empty data, missing keys, malformed detail dicts, string vs dict detail types
- **Output capture tests** (using pytest `capsys`) to verify printed output

**How complex is this work?**
**Complexity: Low-Medium** (2-3 hours)
- These are pure display/formatting functions with side effects (print statements)
- They take simple dict/list inputs and format output
- No external dependencies or complex logic
- Main challenge: setting up proper test data structures for state/plan/issue objects
- Can use pytest's `capsys` fixture for output verification

---

### 2. UNTESTED MODULE: `desloppify/base/discovery/scan.py`

**What the module does:**
This is a thin facade module (118 lines) that acts as the **canonical public API for source file discovery**. It wraps the internal `source.py` module and provides:
- Exclusion management: `set_exclusions()`, `get_exclusions()`
- File cache control: `enable_file_cache()`, `disable_file_cache()`, `is_file_cache_enabled()`
- File reading: `read_file_text(filepath)` with optional caching
- File discovery: `find_source_files()`, `find_py_files()`, `find_ts_files()`, `find_tsx_files()`
- Pattern/extension normalization: `_normalize_patterns()`, `_normalize_extensions()`
- Directory exclusion collection: `collect_exclude_dirs()`

**Location:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/base/discovery/scan.py`

**Current testing status:** **Zero tests** - This module has NO dedicated test coverage. The underlying `source.py` may have some tests, but the facade layer itself is untested.

**What's missing:**
1. No tests for the public API functions
2. No tests for normalization logic (pattern/extension deduplication)
3. No tests for the caching enable/disable lifecycle
4. No tests for path resolution with `collect_exclude_dirs()`
5. No tests for the multi-language file discovery functions (`find_py_files`, `find_ts_files`, `find_tsx_files`)
6. No edge case testing: empty paths, None values, duplicate patterns, malformed extensions

**What testing is needed:**
- **Unit tests for normalization:**
  - `_normalize_patterns()` - test deduplication, empty/whitespace stripping, None handling
  - `_normalize_extensions()` - test `.` prefix addition, case normalization, deduplication
- **Integration tests with source.py:**
  - Test `find_source_files()` returns correct file list
  - Test `find_py_files()` and `find_ts_files()` work with real temp directories
  - Test `collect_exclude_dirs()` respects exclusion patterns
- **Lifecycle tests for file caching:**
  - Test enable/disable toggles the cache state correctly
  - Test `read_file_text()` respects cache state
  - Test cache clearing works
- **Edge cases:** empty lists, paths outside project root, symlinks, missing directories

**How complex is this work?**
**Complexity: Medium** (3-5 hours)
- Requires real filesystem setup (temp directories, test files)
- Need to test normalization logic with various edge cases
- Need to integrate with `source_discovery_mod` to verify delegation works
- File discovery tests need realistic directory structures
- Can use pytest's `tmp_path` fixture for filesystem operations

---

### 3. OVER-MOCKED TEST: `desloppify/tests/commands/test_next_render.py`

**What the module being tested does:**
`render.py` contains the **terminal rendering helpers for the `next` command**. It renders a single item or queue of items with scoring context, dimensions, and auto-fix suggestions. Functions include:
- `render_terminal_items()` - Main orchestrator that routes to different renderers based on item kind
- `_render_item()` - Full item card rendering with type, detail, code snippet, explain info
- `_render_issue_detail()` - Renders file, line numbers, category, importers, suggestion, code snippet
- `_render_score_impact()` - Renders dimension context and impact estimates
- `_render_item_type()` - Renders "Type: Design review" or "Type: Auto-fixable" labels
- Various specific renderers: `_render_workflow_stage()`, `_render_workflow_action()`, `_render_subjective_dimension()`

**Location of tests:** `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/commands/test_next_render.py`

**Current test coverage (3 tests):**

```python
test_render_terminal_items_group_mode_uses_group_renderer()
  - Mocks: _render_grouped (entire function)
  - Tests: routing logic only
  - Missing: actual grouped rendering output

test_render_terminal_items_cluster_drill_renders_compact_followups()
  - Mocks: colorize, _render_item, _render_compact_item (3 functions)
  - Tests: conditional routing (full vs compact item)
  - Missing: actual item rendering, code snippet retrieval, dimension data

test_render_terminal_items_single_item_prints_next_label()
  - Mocks: colorize, _render_item (2 functions)
  - Tests: label output only
  - Missing: complete item card rendering, detail fields
```

**What's over-mocked:**
1. **All rendering functions are mocked away** - Tests only verify that the correct function is called, not that output is formatted correctly
2. **colorize() is mocked to no-op** - This hides whether color styles are applied correctly
3. **_render_grouped, _render_item, _render_compact_item are mocked** - These are the actual work functions, so testing only their invocation is shallow
4. **No integration with render_support module** - The render_support functions (`render_grouped`, `render_compact_item`) that do real work are completely bypassed
5. **No code snippet rendering tested** - `read_code_snippet()` is never called in tests
6. **No dimension scoring tested** - `compute_health_breakdown()` and `compute_score_impact()` are never invoked

**What testing is missing:**
- **Integration tests with actual render_support functions:**
  - Test `_render_grouped()` actually groups items by detector/file correctly
  - Test `_render_item()` with realistic issue data shows all fields
  - Test cluster drill-in logic with real item filtering
- **Output correctness tests:**
  - Verify color codes are applied to output
  - Verify item labels ("Next item", "[#1/5]", etc.) are formatted correctly
  - Verify single vs multi-item rendering differences
- **Field rendering tests:**
  - Test dimension context is printed correctly
  - Test impact estimates are calculated and displayed
  - Test auto-fix batch hints show correct count
  - Test code snippets are retrieved and formatted
  - Test explain mode adds explanation footer
- **Edge cases:**
  - Items with missing fields (no detector, no file, no detail)
  - Confidence levels (high/medium/low)
  - Review dimension vs regular detector items
  - Subjective dimension items
  - Workflow stage/action items

**How complex is this work?**
**Complexity: Medium-High** (4-6 hours)
- Need to create realistic item data structures matching actual state.json issue format
- Tests should use real `render_support` functions, not mock them
- Need to capture and verify printed output using capsys
- Need to mock external dependencies (render_support imports, read_code_snippet) at a deeper level
- Should test integration with scoring functions (compute_health_breakdown, compute_score_impact)
- Consider adding parametrized tests for different item kinds (cluster, workflow_stage, workflow_action, regular issue, review, subjective_dimension)

**Test pattern to follow:**

### 5. OVER-MOCKED TEST: `desloppify/tests/commands/scan/test_plan_reconcile.py`

**What the module being tested does:**
`plan_reconcile.py` is the **post-scan plan reconciliation orchestrator**. After a scan completes, it:
- Loads the plan from disk
- Applies plan reconciliation (handles superseded issues, removes stale items)
- Syncs unscored dimensions into the queue
- Syncs stale subjective dimensions (removes refreshed, adds stale)
- Regenerates auto-clusters
- Seeds/clears plan start scores
- Triggers triage or creates plan workflow if needed
- Saves the plan if any changes occurred

The main entry point is `reconcile_plan_post_scan(runtime)` which orchestrates ~8 different sync functions.

## Summary: Orphaned Module Investigation — `desloppify/base/discovery/scan.py`

### What is `scan.py`?

**The file** is a facade/wrapper layer that sits between callers and the actual implementation in `source.py`. It provides a canonical public API for source file discovery by:

1. **Normalizing inputs** (extensions and exclusion patterns) before delegation
2. **Re-exporting** functions from `source.py` with optional preprocessing
3. **Documenting** the public contract with `__all__`

The file contains these functions:
- **Normalization helpers** (private): `_normalize_patterns()`, `_normalize_extensions()`
- **Public wrappers**: `find_source_files()`, `find_ts_files()`, `find_tsx_files()`, `find_py_files()`
- **Cache management**: `enable_file_cache()`, `disable_file_cache()`, `is_file_cache_enabled()`, etc.
- **Configuration**: `set_exclusions()`, `get_exclusions()`, `collect_exclude_dirs()`

### Is it Truly Orphaned?

**Yes, it is effectively dead code.** Here's why:

**No direct imports anywhere in the codebase:**
```bash
# Comprehensive search found ZERO imports from desloppify.base.discovery.scan
$ grep -r "from desloppify.base.discovery.scan" /desloppify --include="*.py"
$ grep -r "from desloppify.base.discovery import.*scan" /desloppify --include="*.py"
# (both return nothing)
```

**Instead, callers import directly from `source.py`:**
- `cli.py` line 15: `from desloppify.base.discovery.source import set_exclusions`
- `app/commands/scan/workflow.py`: `from desloppify.base.discovery.source import (...)`
- `languages/_framework/generic.py`: `from desloppify.base.discovery.source import find_source_files`
- 76+ other files import from `source.py` directly

**Not exposed in the base/discovery module's public API:**
- `/desloppify/base/discovery/__init__.py` is empty (only 2 lines)
- No re-exports of scan.py or its functions

**Functionally redundant:**
- Both `scan.py` and `source.py` export **identical function signatures** in `__all__`
- `scan.py` only adds input normalization (lowercase extensions, strip whitespace)
- But callers bypass this normalization by importing directly from `source.py`

### Historical Context

**From commit 2e221b4 (F6 directory reorganization):**
- The original file was `desloppify/core/source_scan_api.py` (a standalone facade)
- It was moved to `desloppify/core/discovery/scan.py` as part of module reorganization
- Then renamed again to `base/discovery/scan.py` in commit 2f0d9c2

**The issue is explicitly documented** in a prior analysis run (20260304_033501):
> "Parallel discovery APIs create behavior drift and inconsistent call-site dependencies"
> 
> "`scan.py` is a facade that normalizes extension/pattern inputs before delegation, but `source.py` exposes direct `find_source_files` without that normalization path, yielding different behavior depending on import path... `generic.py` imports `find_source_files` from `base.discovery.source`, bypassing the canonical normalization layer."

### Recommendation

**DELETE `desloppify/base/discovery/scan.py`**

**Reasoning:**
1. **Zero active imports** — no code depends on it
2. **Behavior bypass** — the normalization it provides is ignored because callers import from `source.py` directly
3. **Fits CLAUDE.md policy** — "Remove re-export facades, wrapper shims, and compatibility layers that exist solely so old import paths keep working"
4. **Unifies the API** — eliminates the dual-entrypoint confusion documented in the agent analysis
5. **No migration burden** — there are no callers to update

**Post-deletion verification:**
- All existing imports from `base.discovery.source` will continue working unchanged
- The normalization functions in `scan.py` were never used by real callers anyway
- Tests (76 files mention `find_*_files` functions) all use the functions via `source.py`

**Alternative (NOT recommended):**
If there's concern about the normalization being useful:
- Merge the normalization logic into `source.py`'s `find_source_files()` directly
- Update `source.py`'s `__all__` and imports
- Then delete `scan.py`

But this is unnecessary since the normalization is currently bypassed everywhere.

### Files Affected by Deletion
- `/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/base/discovery/scan.py` (delete)

No other files need changes—there are zero active imports to rewrite.
